Method: Optimizations include circuit refinement from prior work, use of a fast CCZ magic-state factory, depth-one CCZ state injection, non-overlapping measurements, and an efficient loss correction protocol to manage qubit resources.

Result: The optimized logical circuit for ECDLP requires about $1450$ qubits and $40 	imes 10^6$ Toffoli gates, achieving a success probability of at least $1-2^{-128}$, with execution estimated at 25.7 days using 19,397 physical qubits and a $63\\%$ success rate.

Conclusion: The optimizations affirm that a trapped-ion quantum computer can effectively address the ECDLP on $	ext{secp256k1}$, showcasing the potential of the Walking Cat Architecture for practical quantum applications.

Abstract: One of the strengths of our recently proposed Walking Cat Architecture for a trapped-ion quantum computer is that it is straightforward to extend and optimize for a specific application. As a proof-of-concept, here we present such optimizations for solving the $256$-bit elliptic curve discrete logarithm problem (ECDLP) on $\mathtt{secp256k1}$, which is the elliptic curve used by blockchain technologies such as Bitcoin, using Shor's algorithm. We optimize the circuits from Schrottenloher's recent work and arrive at a logical quantum circuit for solving the ECDLP using about $1450$ qubits and $40\cdot 10^6$ Toffoli gates, with a rigorous lower bound on the logical-level success probability that holds with confidence at least $1-2^{-128}$. Using our compilation toolchain with manual optimization of the logical layout and integrated routing, we produce estimates for the logical measurement depth and the required number of physical qubits by compiling all components to measurement schedules that obey the architectural constraints. A key ingredient is a fast CCZ magic-state factory and a depth-one CCZ state injection, reducing the execution time of CCZ gates by a factor of $31$. We increase the logical-measurement parallelism using non-overlapping cat-based measurements in parallel, and we leverage the recently proposed logical CliNR protocol to speed up Clifford operations. To reduce the qubit overhead, we introduce a more efficient loss correction protocol, design a layout that allows us to recycle the CliNR ancilla qubits, and provision reusable cat-state resources according to the circuit's peak measurement parallelism. All results and optimizations combined, we conclude that a trapped-ion quantum computer based on our architecture can solve the ECDLP on $\mathtt{secp256k1}$ in approximately 25.7 days using 19,397 physical qubits with an estimated success probability of $63\%$.

</details>


### [159] [Hawking Radiation in non-Hermitian Microscopic Analogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05632)
*Diego F. Munoz-Arboleda,Cristiane Morais Smith,Marcus Stålhammar*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores a microscopic open quantum system that mimics black-hole dynamics using a fermionic tight-binding chain and Markovian reservoirs, revealing properties akin to black holes and Hawking radiation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge theoretical concepts of black-hole physics with quantum open systems by employing non-Hermitian dynamics.

Method: A fermionic tight-binding chain coupled to Markovian reservoirs is analyzed, leading to a quadratic Lindbladian resulting in non-Hermitian gain/loss and next-nearest-neighbor hopping, establishing analogy with black-hole physics.

Result: The study reveals tilted exceptional cones in the rapidity spectrum and identifies channels consistent with black-hole and white-hole sectors, displaying signatures of Hawking radiation through specific correlations.

Conclusion: The results link microscopic quantum dynamics to emergent geometrical structures, offering insights into analogue Hawking radiation in many-body systems.

Abstract: Non-Hermitian systems have recently emerged as a convenient platform to realize black-hole physics. Here, we propose a microscopic open quantum system, namely a fermionic tight-binding chain coupled to Markovian reservoirs, which emulates black-hole properties. The resulting quadratic Lindbladian admits an effective non-Hermitian limit that produces a gain and loss lattice model with a non-reciprocal next-nearest-neighbor hopping. The rapidity spectrum of the full Lindbladian forms tilted exceptional cones that define an effective Painlevé-Gullstrand geometry and separates into black-hole and white-hole sectors, while steady-state particle densities and currents retain clear signatures at the horizon positions. We then analyze the effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonian that arise from the microscopic quantum system. The biorthogonal flux identifies the outgoing exterior and interior channels. Finally, we introduce a fermionic Gaussian Nambu extension and formulate Hawking-pair witnesses through frequency-resolved scattering, nonlocal density-density correlations, and anomalous Hawking-partner amplitudes. The resulting correlations display analogue Hawking radiation signatures, while the frequency-resolved Hawking-partner correlations satisfy the fermionic covariance-positivity constraint. These results establish a connection between microscopic open-system dynamics, emergent non-Hermitian geometry, and fermionic many-body probes of analogue Hawking radiation.

</details>


### [160] [$N$-dimensional discrete Fourier transform via bosonic Hamiltonian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05644)
*Edgar Barriga,Camila Muñoz,Alejandro Muñoz,Santiago Rojas-Rojas,Pablo Solano,Carla Hermann-Avigliano,Aldo Delgado*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novel approach to implement the N-dimensional discrete Fourier transform (DFT) in photonic integrated circuits using bosonic Hamiltonians, significantly reducing the complexity compared to traditional methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the limitations of current architectures that use cascades of Mach-Zehnder interferometers for N-dimensional DFT implementations, which can be complex and inefficient.

Method: The authors propose using bosonic Hamiltonians for a single-stage multimode evolution complemented by phase shifters, modeled as graphs where edges are couplings and vertices are waveguides, allowing analytical and numerical solutions for DFT up to N=31.

Result: The research reports all solutions for N≤8, partial results for N=9, and selected configurations for N=10, along with conjectures for N≥11. A sensitivity criterion is also established to identify suitable waveguide layouts.

Conclusion: The proposed method achieves an O(N log log N) scaling for DFT implementation, enabling the construction of a 2520-dimensional DFT with only 2625 interferometers, vastly improving on previous architectures.

Abstract: The discrete Fourier transform (DFT) underpins many classical algorithms and is a fundamental unitary operator for quantum information processing. Implementing the $N$-dimensional DFT in photonic integrated circuits (PICs) is limited by the cascades of Mach-Zehnder interferometers that current architectures require. Here we propose bosonic Hamiltonians that realize the $N$-dimensional DFT through a single stage of multimode evolution, complemented only by phase shifters before and after the interaction region, in a geometry suited to 3D waveguides. Modeling the system as a graph, where edges correspond to couplings and the vertices are the waveguides, we obtain analytical solutions for complete graphs up to $N=6$ and numerical solutions up to $N=31$. For non-complete graphs, different propagation constants are required in the Hamiltonian. We report all solutions for $N\leq 8$, partial exploration for $N=9$, and selected cases for $N=10$, together with three conjectures that guide the numerical search for $N \geq 11$. These configurations circumvent the vanishing evanescent coupling strength imposed by the waveguide separation, and a closed-form sensitivity criterion selects those that are admissible as a waveguide layout and least sensitive to fabrication error. We also uncover the missing non-affine parameters of the $6$-dimensional DFT, and show that the scaling law for implementing the $N$-dimensional DFT with our building blocks is $\mathcal{O}(N\log\log{N})$. This allows assembling the $2520$-dimensional DFT with only $2625$ interferometers, in contrast to the $\approx 3\times 10^6$ of Reck and Clements architectures.

</details>


### [161] [Ordered-Angle Coding for Exact Multiuser Unanimity Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05645)
*Luis Adrián Lizama-Pérez*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents ordered-angle coding for efficient binary-unanimity testing among users in a serial quantum setup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the efficiency and reliability of binary-unanimity tests in a quantum communication setting.

Method: Introduced ordered-angle coding and established deterministic conditions for pulse generation based on user sign choices, leveraging a mixed Hamming weight to optimize the unanimous signature representation.

Result: A finite perfect word exists for binary-unanimity testing if and only if the number of users n is a power of two, leading to efficient verification methods reducing worst-case complexities.

Conclusion: The study offers robust bounds for operations, allowing for practical implementations, while framing it as a coded relation primitive suitable for conference key generation.

Abstract: We introduce ordered-angle coding for binary-unanimity testing among $n$ transformation-only users in a serial quantum architecture inherited from Loop-Back communication. User $B_i$ fixes one private sign across a logical word and applies $R(s_iα_j)$ in trial $j$. If $w$ users choose the negative sign, serial composition gives $R[(n-2w)α_j]$. We show that every fixed same-axis pulse that is deterministic for both unanimous inputs under the binary Bell readout has $α_j=q_jπ/(2n)$ with integer $q_j$. For a nonadaptive word $\mathbf q=(q_1,\ldots,q_m)$, a mixed Hamming weight imitates the unanimous signature with probability \[ M_{\mathbf q}(w)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\cos^2\!\left(\frac{πq_jw}{n}\right). \] Within this complete deterministic-unanimity pulse family, a finite perfect word exists if and only if $n$ is a power of two. For $n=2^r$, the dyadic word $(1,2,4,\ldots,2^{r-1})$ is exact and pulse-minimal with $m_{\min}=r=\log_2n$ trials. It replaces the $O(n^2\log(1/\varepsilon))$ worst-case burden of repeated smallest-angle tests by exact $O(\log n)$ verification. Odd primes instead admit balanced statistical words with uniform mixed-weight imitation $2^{-(p-1)}$.
  For actual operations $R(s_iα_j+δ_{ij})$, an ideal rejecting position is lifted to $\sin^2Δ_j$, where $Δ_j=\sum_iδ_{ij}$. This yields explicit robustness bounds with angular and binary-readout errors. Bell entanglement is not required for the additive algebra, but it keeps the traveler locally maximally mixed in every honest trial. We therefore present the result as a coded multiuser relation primitive with optional raw conference-key-candidate use, not as a composably secure conference-key protocol.

</details>


### [162] [Minimum Cardinalities of Multipartite Unextendible Product Bases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05657)
*Chenhao Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes a theorem regarding unextendible product bases (UPBs) in multipartite quantum systems, specifically addressing cases that prevent attainment of the lower bound for UPB size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research aims to resolve the minimum cardinality problem for unextendible product bases (UPBs) in multipartite quantum systems, which is crucial in understanding quantum entanglement.

Method: The authors develop a proof based on a unified graph-theoretic framework to address the conditions under which the minimum number of pairwise orthogonal product states differs from the established lower bound.

Result: They prove that for any non-all-qubit system with at least three components, if parity issues prevent the lower bound from being reached, the minimum UPB size is exactly one more than the lower bound.

Conclusion: This work resolves the long-standing minimum-cardinality problem for UPBs in all finite quantum systems, indicating clear conditions under which the optimal number of orthogonal product states can be determined.

Abstract: In quantum information theory, the state space of a multipartite quantum system is modeled by a tensor product. In the tensor-product space $\mathbb C^{d_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb C^{d_p}$, a nonzero vector is a \emph{product state} if it can be written as $\lvert \varphi_1\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes\lvert \varphi_p\rangle$ with $\lvert \varphi_j\rangle\in\mathbb C^{d_j}\setminus\{0\}$. An \emph{unextendible product basis} (UPB) is a finite family of pairwise orthogonal product states such that no nonzero product state is orthogonal to all of them. UPBs play a key role in investigating quantum entanglement and nonlocal phenomena. Finding a smallest UPB is a natural extremal problem: it asks how few pairwise orthogonal product states suffice to prevent any further product state from being added. The general minimum-size problem for UPBs has been studied for over two decades since the seminal work of Alon and Lovász. For local dimensions $d_1,\ldots,d_p\ge2$, let $f_m(d_1,\ldots,d_p)$ be the minimum cardinality of a UPB and let $f_{LB}(d_1,\ldots,d_p)=1+\sum_{j=1}^{p}(d_j-1)$ be the natural lower bound. Alon and Lovász determined exactly when $f_m$ attains the lower bound $f_{LB}$, but the obstructed multipartite cases remained open in general.
  We prove a stabilization theorem: for every non-all-qubit system with $p\ge3$, whenever parity prevents the natural lower bound $f_{LB}$ from being attained, the true minimum is exactly $f_{LB}+1$. Equivalently, if the number of even local dimensions is positive and even, and at least one local dimension is greater than two, then $f_m(d_1,\ldots,d_p)=f_{LB}(d_1,\ldots,d_p)+1$. The proof is built on a unified graph-theoretic framework. Our result, together with earlier work, settles the minimum-cardinality problem for UPBs in all finite quantum systems.

</details>


### [163] [Robustness of Dynamical Casimir Effect-Induced Quantum Synchronization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05659)
*Sebastián Francisquez,Fernando C. Lombardo,Paula I. Villar*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the robustness of quantum synchronization induced by the dynamical Casimir effect (DCE) in a circuit quantum electrodynamics system, assessing decoherence impacts and confirming experimental feasibility under realistic conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the effects of decoherence on quantum synchronization in cQED systems, specifically looking at the dynamical Casimir effect as a mechanism for synchronization.

Method: A Lindblad master equation approach is employed to evaluate synchronization dynamics in the presence of various decoherence channels, including photon loss and pure dephasing.

Result: The study finds that DCE-induced synchronization remains feasible under realistic dissipation rates; pure dephasing significantly limits synchronization fidelity, and environmental relaxation can create misleading classical correlations.

Conclusion: The findings provide crucial insights into the parameter regimes for observing genuine DCE-induced quantum synchronization and practical guidelines for future cQED experiments.

Abstract: The robustness of quantum synchronization induced by the dynamical Casimir effect (DCE) is analyzed within a circuit quantum electrodynamics (cQED) architecture consisting of two superconducting qubits coupled to a shared, parametrically driven cavity. Using a Lindblad master equation approach, this study evaluates the impact of various decoherence channels (including photon loss, relaxation, thermal excitation, and pure dephasing) on the resulting synchronization dynamics. The results demonstrate that DCE-induced synchronization persists under dissipation rates achievable in state-of-the-art superconducting platforms, confirming its experimental feasibility. Among the studied mechanisms, pure dephasing is identified as the dominant factor limiting the fidelity of the synchronized state. Furthermore, it is shown that in strongly dissipative regimes, the Pearson correlation coefficient may overestimate the degree of quantum synchronization, as environmental relaxation can generate classical correlations that mimic synchronized behavior. These findings establish the parameter regimes necessary for observing genuine DCE-induced quantum synchronization and provide practical guidelines for future cQED experiments.

</details>


### [164] [Kerr Induced Control of Synchronization and Quantum State Recovery in a Driven van der Pol Oscillator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05672)
*Amir Hossein Houshmand Almani,Ali Mortezapour,Alireza Nourmandipour*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper explores the impact of Kerr nonlinearity on quantum synchronization in a squeezed van der Pol oscillator, revealing how it alters dynamics and photon statistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the interplay between Kerr nonlinearity and quantum synchronization in squeezed oscillator systems, and its implications for quantum technologies.

Method: Analysis of amplitude-dependent frequency shifts leading to saddle-node bifurcations, alongside global synchronization phase diagrams in the squeezing-Kerr parameter space.

Result: Kerr nonlinearity induces a transition from bistable to monostable dynamics, evident in systematic frequency pulling, spectral broadening, and a distinct synchronization boundary not aligned with photon statistics crossover.

Conclusion: The findings establish design principles for manipulating quantum synchronization via Kerr nonlinearity, applicable to various quantum platforms.

Abstract: We investigate how Kerr nonlinearity modifies quantum synchronization in a squeezed quantum van der Pol oscillator. We show that the Kerr interaction produces an amplitude-dependent frequency shift that drives a saddle-node bifurcation, transforming the classical phase-space structure from bistable to monostable dynamics. In the quantum regime, this transition manifests as systematic frequency pulling and spectral broadening, while the steady-state Wigner function reveals a continuous correspondence between the quantum state and the semiclassical attractor despite finite quantum fluctuations. By constructing global synchronization phase diagrams in the squeezing--Kerr parameter space, we uncover a remarkably linear dependence of the critical squeezing strength required to maintain phase locking on the Kerr nonlinearity. We further demonstrate that the synchronization boundary does not coincide with the crossover between super- and sub-Poissonian photon statistics, showing that synchronization and photon-number statistics characterize distinct aspects of the quantum steady state. These results provide quantitative design principles for controlling quantum synchronization through Kerr nonlinearity, with potential relevance to trapped-ion, superconducting-circuit, and optomechanical platforms.

</details>


### [165] [Quantum lower bounds for convex optimization and real matrix-vector query problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05679)
*Andrew M. Childs*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes that the quantum query complexity for minimizing convex functions in $	ext{R}^n$ is $	ildeΩ(n)$, matching known upper bounds, and introduces a new technique for proving lower bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the quantum query complexity for convex function minimization has implications for quantum algorithms and computational complexity.

Method: The authors utilize a novel quantum lower bound technique called the determinantal witness method, focusing on Fourier transforms and low-rank matrices.

Result: The paper demonstrates lower bounds of $	ildeΩ(n)$ for convex function minimization and presents linear or nearly linear lower bounds for computing properties of matrices like trace and determinant.

Conclusion: This work advances the field of quantum complexity by providing tight bounds and a new technique for deriving lower bounds in quantum queries.

Abstract: We (the author and the AI systems that did the heavy lifting) show that the quantum query complexity of minimizing a convex function over a convex subset of $\mathbb{R}^n$ with evaluation and membership queries is $\tildeΩ(n)$, nearly matching the best known upper bound. In particular, we show this even for quadratic minimization, which is equivalent to inverting an $n \times n$ real matrix using matrix-vector queries. We also show linear or nearly linear lower bounds on the quantum query complexity of computing the trace, the sign of the determinant, and the magnitude of the determinant of a real matrix in the matrix-vector query model. We use a novel quantum lower bound technique, the determinantal witness method, based on identifying a witness whose Fourier transform vanishes on low-rank matrices and that correlates well with the function being computed.

</details>


### [166] [Characterizing Privacy Risks of Quantum Machine Learning with Emergent Quantum-Native Access](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05702)
*Liou Tang,James Joshi,Ashish Kundu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper addresses privacy risks in Quantum Machine Learning (QML), focusing on membership inference attacks in a quantum-native context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need to evaluate privacy risks in QML emerges from its rapid development and the unique threats posed by quantum capabilities, challenging existing privacy-preserving methods.

Method: The paper examines membership inference attacks in QML models, assessing both theoretical privacy leakage and empirical adversarial exploitability in a scenario where users have quantum capabilities.

Result: Findings reveal that traditional models underestimate privacy risks when quantum users are involved, highlighting a disparity between theoretical predictions and practical outcomes due to QML's probabilistic nature.

Conclusion: The results indicate that there is a need for a deeper examination of privacy vulnerabilities in QML within quantum-native contexts, establishing a foundation for future research.

Abstract: Quantum Machine Learning (QML) has shown rapid advances by utilizing quantum computing for machine learning tasks. Meanwhile, the privacy risks accompanying QML is also starting to be studied, which inherit privacy leakage channels from "classical" ML and also quantum-unique risks. Existing work on privacy-preserving QML largely focuses on a QML-as-a-service scenario, which generally assumes that the QML model owner provides only classical bit outputs to queries, while users (and adversaries) have only classical computing abilities. However, this view is increasingly challenged in a quantum-native world of quantum-capable users/adversaries, which may have access to both quantum computing abilities and access to quantum information output from service providers.
  In this paper, we aim to bridge this gap by examining membership inference attacks against QML models by demonstrating that increasing quantum access and quantum computing abilities provides provable theoretical privacy leakage and empirical adversarial gain. However, the probabilistic nature of QML introduces a gap between theoretical and empirical adversarial advantage. These results show that existing research on privacy leakage on QML models underestimate privacy leakage in an emergent quantum-native access regimes, which we hope to establish a first step into examining potential privacy leakages for QML in the quantum-native world.These results show that existing research on privacy leakage in QML models underestimates privacy leakage in emergent quantum-native access regimes, and we hope to establish a first step in examining potential privacy leakages for QML in the quantum-native world.

</details>


### [167] [Tight Lower Bounds for State Tomography with Limited Entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05718)
*Ufuk Keskin,Jason Luo,Mahbod Majid,Matthew Radzihovsky*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes lower and upper bounds on copy complexity for state tomography when measurements act on multiple fresh copies without retaining quantum memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To better understand the resources required for estimating quantum states in state tomography, especially when the number of copies used for measurement increases.

Method: A Fisher information bound is utilized for one measurement block, which relies on the smallest eigenvalue of the state, alongside Fano's inequality and log-Sobolev comparisons to derive results for adaptive protocols.

Result: The copy complexity is determined to be $	ext{max}\\{d^3/(\\sqrt{k}ε^2), d^2/ε^2\\}$, effectively providing a generalized lower bound applicable to various measurement scenarios.

Conclusion: The results extend previous work by removing the limitation on $k$, making the findings more broadly applicable to different measurement strategies.

Abstract: We study state tomography when each measurement acts on at most $k$ fresh copies and no quantum memory is retained between blocks. We prove a lower bound matching the upper bound in [arXiv:2510.07788]. Thus the copy complexity of estimating an arbitrary $d$-dimensional state to trace distance $ε$ is, up to absolute constant factors, $\max\{d^3/(\sqrt{k}ε^2),d^2/ε^2\}$ for every $k$ and all sufficiently small $ε$. This removes the earlier restriction that $k$ be small as a function of the accuracy. The lower bound applies to arbitrary measurements within each block and adaptive choices between blocks. The lower bound already applies in a small neighborhood of any state whose smallest eigenvalue is of order $1/d$, even when the center is known.
  The main ingredient is a uniform Fisher information bound for one measurement block that depends only on the smallest eigenvalue of the state. The proof avoids the perturbative expansion responsible for the restriction in [arXiv:2402.16353]. Fano's inequality for metric balls and a log-Sobolev comparison between mutual and Fisher information then reduce the adaptive protocol to this block bound [arXiv:1607.00550, arXiv:1902.08582].

</details>


### [168] [SAQC: A SAT-Aware Compilation Framework for QAOA-Based Quantum Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05737)
*Till Schnittka,Julie Maria Raju,Abhoy Kole,Rolf Drechsler*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: SAQC introduces a SAT-aware compilation framework for optimizing the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) in solving SAT problems, preserving Boolean structure, and improving compilation efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance the efficiency of QAOA for SAT by preserving essential Boolean information during the compilation process, which is lost in traditional approaches.

Method: SAQC uses a SAT Intermediate Representation (SIR) to maintain Boolean clause structure, supports CNF and eCNF formulations, and implements optimizations like clause rewriting and dependency analysis before quantum lowering.

Result: SAQC shows improved performance with significant reductions in circuit depth, two-qubit gate counts, and compilation time compared to traditional Hamiltonian-based methods.

Conclusion: The framework effectively integrates SAT-specific optimizations into QAOA compilation, making it a robust tool that advances quantum computing applications for SAT problems.

Abstract: The Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) is a promising variational approach for solving Boolean satisfiability (SAT) problems. Existing SAT-to-QAOA workflows first translate Boolean formulas into penalty Hamiltonians before applying generic quantum compilation, thereby discarding SAT-specific information such as clause structure, logical operators, and variable dependencies. Consequently, subsequent compiler optimizations cannot exploit the underlying Boolean formulation. This paper presents SAQC, a SAT-aware compilation framework that introduces a SAT Intermediate Representation (SIR) to preserve Boolean structure during compilation. The proposed framework supports both CNF and XOR-extended CNF (eCNF) formulations and performs SAT-aware optimizations including clause rewriting, dependency analysis, and clause scheduling prior to quantum lowering. Building on the optimized SIR, SAQC provides a unified framework for both penalty Hamiltonian generation and direct clause-to-ansatz synthesis, together with ansatz optimizations based on relative-phase decomposition, ancilla-assisted synthesis, and dynamic uncomputation. Experimental results demonstrate significant reductions in circuit depth, two-qubit gate count, and compilation time compared with conventional Hamiltonian-based workflows while remaining compatible with existing quantum compilation frameworks.

</details>


### [169] [Quantum Query Algorithms for the Constructive Diagonal Ramsey Theorem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05812)
*Cheng Xin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a quantum algorithm for finding homogeneous sets in graphs, improving the efficiency over classical methods and establishing new lower bounds for Ramsey-related problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To find efficient algorithms for the constructive diagonal Ramsey problem, leveraging quantum computing's advantages over classical approaches.

Method: A bounded-error quantum algorithm that uses a recursion with adjacency constraints and quantum search techniques to sample candidate sets while balancing accuracy and cost.

Result: Achieves homogeneous set recovery with sublinear query complexity, notably $O(rac{√N}{	ext{log}(	ext{log}N/η)})$, outperforming classical algorithms.

Conclusion: The proposed algorithm offers a significant polynomial quantum speedup, particularly for random graph cases, and establishes new lower bounds for quantum search in Ramsey problems.

Abstract: The constructive diagonal Ramsey problem asks, given adjacency-oracle access to an $N$-vertex graph, for a clique or independent set of the order guaranteed by Ramsey's theorem. We give a bounded-error quantum algorithm that, for every $K\ge2$ and $N\ge4^{K-1}$, finds and verifies a homogeneous $K$-set using $O\!\left(2^K K\log\frac Kη\right)$ edge queries with failure probability at most $η$. At the Ramsey scale $N=2^n$, this yields a homogeneous set of order $\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1$ using $O(\sqrt N\log N\log(\log N/η))$ queries, improving on the $O(N)$ queries of the explicit classical recursion and giving, to our knowledge, the first sublinear worst-case algorithm for the Ramsey relation. We also derive an $Ω(N^{1/12})$ quantum lower bound by a reduction from collision finding.
  The algorithm runs the constructive recursion over implicit candidate sets. Each set is represented by a short conjunction of adjacency constraints and sampled using capped unknown-solution quantum search, and a scale-aware concentration schedule balances estimation accuracy against the increasing cost of sampling deeper sets. We complement the upper bound with an $Ω(N^{1-1/\sqrt2})$ randomized lower bound, transported from the random-Painter analysis of online Ramsey numbers, which holds on the uniform distribution $G(N,1/2)$. On that distribution a greedy quantum search uses only $\widetilde O(N^{1/4})$ queries, giving a provable polynomial quantum speedup for Ramsey search on random graphs. We also give an estimation-free size-biased recursion and extend it to every fixed number of edge colours.

</details>


### [170] [Quantum Query Complexity of Persistence Statistics in Graph Zigzags](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05813)
*Cheng Xin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores the query complexity involved in estimating scalar summaries of bar lifetimes in graphs using quantum and classical methods, revealing optimal sample complexities for different regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The goal is to improve query complexity for estimating scalar summaries of zigzag bar lifetimes from limited access to graph snapshots, particularly using quantum computing techniques.

Method: The proposed algorithm leverages an exact identity related to sampling uniform times in graphs and computes generalized ranks related to zigzag structures, offering a quantum estimator with specific query complexities.

Result: The quantum estimator provides lower query complexities compared to classical approaches under various conditions, with optimal complexities established for fixed weights and sparse instances.

Conclusion: The findings suggest that quantum estimators can significantly outperform classical methods in terms of query efficiency for certain graph structures, while also maintaining performance under adaptive conditions.

Abstract: We study the query complexity of estimating scalar summaries of zigzag bar lifetimes from snapshot-adjacency bits.
  For graphs $G_1,\ldots,G_m$ on $n$ labeled vertices, let $\ell_b$ be the snapshot lifetime of a degree-one bar $b$ of the intersection zigzag. For a probability generating function $φ(x)=\mathbb{E}[x^R]$, the statistic $F_φ=\sum_bφ(\ell_b/m)$ includes normalized degree-$r$ total persistence and the mean generalized rank over a uniform time window. An exact identity underlies our algorithm: sample $R$ uniform times; the expected generalized rank between their minimum and maximum equals $F_φ$. For graphs that rank is the circuit rank of an intersection graph, so a nonlinear barcode functional becomes an average of edge and component counts, and no barcode is computed.
  Without spectral-gap, homology-state, or QRAM assumptions, this gives a quantum estimator with additive error $\varepsilon n$ and $\widetilde O(\sqrt{m(K+n)}/\varepsilon)$ queries when a bound $K\ge F_φ$ is supplied, against $\widetilde O(m\min\{n^2,(K+n)/\varepsilon^2\})$ classically, and an adaptive quantum variant with the same instance dependence. These estimators are optimal in two regimes. For every fixed power weight $x^r$, $r\ge2$, and for the uniform-window mean, the worst-case complexities are $\widetildeΘ(n\sqrt m/\varepsilon)$ quantum and $Θ(n^2m)$ classical. On sparse instances, under an explicit split-leakage promise met by power and binomial weights of logarithmic degree and the promise $F_φ\le K$, they are $\widetildeΘ(\sqrt{mK}/\varepsilon)$ and $\widetildeΘ(m\min\{n^2,K/\varepsilon^2\})$. The classical lower bounds hold against fully adaptive algorithms, and fewer than $m$ such statistics cannot determine the positive-lifetime histogram. All bounds concern snapshot access; with an explicit update stream, near-linear full-barcode algorithms are known.

</details>


### [171] [Towards Continuous Profiling and Optimization of Quantum-Classical Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05814)
*Ayush Bansal,Owen Cochell,Santiago Núñez-Corrales,Marcos Frenkel,Seetharami Seelam,Apoorve Mohan,Tianyin Xu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: LLQM is a meta-framework that optimizes quantum-classical pipelines by profiling resource dependencies and interactions across stages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limitations of existing frameworks that treat quantum-classical pipeline stages as static and isolated, despite their diverse resource requirements and stochastic behavior.

Method: LLQM decomposes quantum-classical pipelines into fine-grained tasks and continuously profiles their resource dependencies (CPU/GPU, memory, QPU, and queue) in real-time, capturing cross-stage interactions.

Result: Evaluation on IBM's Heron r2 processors demonstrated that continuous profiling identifies runtime bottlenecks and supports optimizations that consider hardware, fidelity, and workload.

Conclusion: LLQM enhances the efficiency of quantum-classical pipelines by enabling more informed resource management and optimizations based on real-time data.

Abstract: Quantum applications increasingly execute as multi-stage quantum-classical pipelines, interleaving QPU computation with classical stages like circuit generation, transpilation, layout mapping, quantum error mitigation (QEM), and post-processing. These stages have diverse resource requirements and exhibit stochastic behavior under drifting hardware noises, yet existing workflow frameworks treat them as static, isolated components. We present LLQM (Low-Level Quantum Machine), a profiling-driven meta-framework for quantum-classical pipelines. LLQM decomposes pipelines into fine-grained tasks and continuously profiles their CPU/GPU, memory, QPU, and queue dependencies alongside real-time hardware states. This unified runtime abstraction captures cross-stage resource dependencies and reveals how classical and quantum decisions interact, enabling characterization of their impact on fidelity and resource consumption. We evaluate LLQM using QEM as a representative pipeline stage, on IBM 156-qubit Heron r2 processors with circuits up to 100 qubits and 1e7 transpiled gates. Our results show that continuous profiling exposes runtime bottlenecks and enables hardware-, fidelity-, and workload-aware optimizations.

</details>


### [172] [Topologically Protected Edge States in One-Dimensional Quantum Walks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05823)
*Emily Maxey,Jacob Mansfield,Beth Thacker,Wade DeGottardi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores topological quantum walks with variable step lengths, revealing insights into their topological classifications and edge states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The robustness of boundary states in topological insulators makes them suitable for applications, prompting the exploration of their realization through discrete-time quantum walks.

Method: The authors introduce a novel class of topological quantum walks characterized by variable step lengths, employing a transfer-matrix approach to analyze edge states and their properties.

Result: The study finds that these quantum walks can exhibit higher winding numbers and multiple edge states, and predicts their spatial profiles and spin structures, showing good agreement with numerical simulations.

Conclusion: This research lays the groundwork for designing quantum walks with enhanced control over topological edge states, aiding future applications in quantum technology.

Abstract: Topological insulators host protected boundary states that are robust to disorder, making them attractive for applications and motivating their realization in engineered systems. Discrete-time quantum walks, which have been implemented in a variety of experimental platforms, can exhibit topologically nontrivial phases and their associated boundary states. Here, we introduce a class of topological quantum walks with variable step lengths that offer theoretical insights into the topological classification of such walks. For example, these walks can access higher winding numbers and support multiple edge states. They also provide context for the reduction of topological protection from a $\mathbb{Z}$- to $\mathbb{Z}_2$-valued invariant when time-reversal symmetry is broken. The topologically protected edge states are investigated using a transfer-matrix approach that describes their spatial profiles and spin structures. These predictions are in excellent agreement with numerical results and, where applicable, with the Jackiw--Rebbi zero-mode solution of the Dirac equation. Taken together, our analysis provides a roadmap for designing quantum walks with control over the number, spatial profile, and spin structure of their topological edge states.

</details>


### [173] [On the sample complexity of the generalized hidden shift problem over arbitrary finite groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05828)
*Juntai Zhou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper addresses the generalized hidden shift problem in finite groups, providing bounds on sample complexity related to group theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance understanding of the hidden shift problem in the context of finite group structures and data requirements.

Method: Formulation of the problem over arbitrary finite groups and analysis to derive bounds.

Result: Establishment of upper and lower bounds for sample complexity based on group theoretic parameters.

Conclusion: The findings contribute to clarifying the complexity dependencies in the generalized hidden shift problem.

Abstract: We formulate the generalized hidden shift problem over arbitrary finite groups and obtain upper and lower bounds for the sample complexity in terms of group theoretic data.

</details>


### [174] [High-Resolution Dynamical Eigenspectroscopy via Variational Trotter Compression on a Superconducting Qubit Processor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05838)
*Liyang Sui,Xingrui Liu,Yufan Li,Sainan Huai,Zhiwen Zong,Kunliang Bu,Xiaopei Yang,Wenyan Jin,Bowen Chen,Xutao Zhang,Jianlan Wu,Shengyu Zhang,Yi Yin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a dynamical eigenspectroscopy protocol that enhances energy structure extraction on noisy quantum devices using Variational Trotter Compression, achieving high accuracy in energy retrieval for quantum systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study addresses the limitations of high-resolution eigenspectroscopy in noisy quantum devices due to circuit depth and coherence time constraints.

Method: A dynamical eigenspectroscopy protocol is introduced, utilizing Variational Trotter Compression to extend unitary evolution duration, along with time-domain autocorrelation reconstruction via quantum state tomography on a 9-qubit superconducting processor.

Result: Eigenenergies are extracted with a precision of 2x10^-3, matching exact-diagonalization values, even in strongly interacting regimes.

Conclusion: The experimental approach proves viable for simulating quantum chemistry and correlated lattice systems, effectively connecting weak and strong coupling regimes.

Abstract: The pursuit of high-resolution eigenspectroscopy on noisy intermediate-scale quantum devices is often hindered by the trade-off between circuit depth and coherence time. In this work, we introduce and experimentally demonstrate a dynamical eigenspectroscopy protocol that extracts fine-grained energy structures from time-dependent survival amplitudes. To overcome the finite coherence window of current superconducting processors, we employ Variational Trotter Compression (VTC) as a practical means to extend the duration of high-fidelity unitary evolution. Using a multi-connected 9-qubit superconducting processor, we reconstruct the time-domain autocorrelation signal via quantum state tomography for the H2 molecule at different bond lengths and for the Fermi-Hubbard model across different correlation regimes. Through multi-frequency fitting and Fourier analysis, the extracted eigenenergies agree with the exact-diagonalization values to within 2x10^-3, including the near-degenerate levels in the strongly interacting regime. Our results establish experimental dynamical spectroscopy as a robust and generalizable framework for simulating both quantum chemistry and strongly correlated lattice systems, bridging weak- and strong-coupling regimes on near-term quantum hardware.

</details>


### [175] [Do Quantum AIs Dream in Paths? Path-Integral Slow Thinking through Grover Interference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05842)
*Xiansheng Cai,Xiu-Hao Deng,Kun Chen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper explores quantum AI's ability to achieve slow thinking in reinforcement learning through coherent reasoning trajectories, using Grover's algorithm to enhance training dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether quantum AI can offer a superior approach to reinforcement learning, specifically in achieving slow thinking while avoiding policy collapse seen in classical methods.

Method: The authors model slow thinking as a coherent dynamics framework using a discrete path integral approach, incorporating Grover amplitude amplification for improving decision-making accuracy in reinforcement learning tasks.

Result: In experiments with a sliding puzzle task, quantum training achieved higher accuracy (0.95) compared to classical methods (0.73), and demonstrated better held-out accuracy and efficiency in supporting training questions.

Conclusion: The implementation of Grover-based path-integral techniques successfully fosters exploratory diversity and verifies performance in reinforcement learning, showcasing the potential of quantum methods over classical counterparts.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards enables large language models to think slowly, but the same training can induce policy collapse: probability concentrates onto a few successful trajectories and exploratory diversity erodes. We ask whether quantum AI can realize slow thinking differently. We formulate slow thinking as coherent dynamics over reasoning trajectories, a discrete path integral in which action sequences coexist in superposition and recombine before measurement. In our trainable realization, an exact verifier partitions the ensemble into collective accepted and rejected components that interfere under Grover amplitude amplification. A finite Grover evolution is maximized when the pre-amplification success probability lies at an analytically determined value below one, so inference itself defines an interior training target and removes the monotonic pressure toward unit success. In exact statevector simulations of a 2x3 sliding puzzle, Grover training reaches accuracy 0.95 on a 32-question training set at one round, against 0.73 for the strongest classical control. On held-out questions specialization has a cost: an untrained uniform policy read out through the same amplification remains the strongest reference on this solution-dense benchmark, and quantum training preserves far more held-out accuracy than classical training - at four rounds with matched circuit applications the two quantum models reach 3.2 and 3.9 times the strongest classical controls. The number of training questions supported by fixed-size policies trained at each amplification budget also grows faster with the budget than with matched classical repetition. These results establish a Grover-based realization of path-integral slow thinking: the interior target preserves exploratory path diversity, and ensemble-level interference converts it into verified performance.

</details>


### [176] [Quantum Message Passing Convergence and Vanishing Block-Error Probability for Random LDPC Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05857)
*Avijit Mandal,Christophe Piveteau,Joseph M. Renes,Henry D. Pfister*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a two-stage BPQM decoder for random $q$-ary LDPC codes, proving that its block-error probability vanishes as blocklength increases, in contrast to previous tree-based methods. It employs a depth-$ackslashell$ approach to manage decoding errors and extends results to irregular ensembles, affirming the decoder's relevance in quantum algorithms like DQI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve decoding effectiveness for LDPC codes with cycles using BPQM while ensuring error probabilities diminish with larger block lengths, addressing limitations of existing tree-based analyses.

Method: A two-stage BPQM decoder is constructed for random $q$-ary LDPC codes, using depth-$ackslashell$ decoding to manage tree neighborhoods and treating other coordinates as erasures combined with noncommutative union bounds for error control.

Result: The average block-error probability approaches zero as block length $N$ increases; fidelity bounds show double-exponential decay of symbol-error probability for regular ensembles with $d_v \geq 3$.

Conclusion: Results validate BPQM's application in quantum coding algorithms like DQI, ensuring effective decoding for specific random LDPC codes over pure-state channels.

Abstract: Belief propagation with quantum messages (BPQM) is a quantum algorithm that decodes classical codes transmitted over classical--quantum channels. It realizes optimal decoding on tree factor graphs over pure-state classical-quantum channels. However, this tree-based analysis does not ensure vanishing block-error probability for LDPC Tanner graphs with cycles. In this work, we construct a two-stage BPQM decoder for random $q$-ary LDPC codes over symmetric $q$-ary pure-state channels, where $q$ is prime, and prove that its ensemble-average block-error probability vanishes as the blocklength $N$ tends to infinity. For regular ensembles with $d_v\geq3$, fidelity bounds yield double-exponential decay of the average symbol-error probability throughout the BPQM success region. We apply depth-$\ell$ BPQM to coordinates with tree neighbourhoods and treat the remaining coordinates as erasures. With a suitable $\ell=Θ(\log\log N)$, a noncommutative union bound controls the BPQM decoding errors, while the minimum-distance property guarantees erasure recovery. We also extend the analysis to finite-support irregular ensembles. These results are relevant to quantum algorithms based on Regev's reduction, where coherent decoding uncomputes a codeword register. Decoded quantum interferometry (DQI) uses a closely related Fourier-based framework that reduces sparse max-LINSAT optimization problems to LDPC decoding problems on pure-state channels. Our results justify the use of BPQM in the decoding step of DQI and of coding-theoretic algorithms based on Regev's reduction whenever the code is drawn from one of the random LDPC ensembles analyzed here and the induced memoryless symmetric pure-state channel lies in the BPQM success region.

</details>


### [177] [Demailly's Conjecture in Quantum Language and Its Solution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05866)
*Trung Hoa Dinh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes a correspondence between fat-point interpolation in complex projective space and dark-state problems in bosonic systems, proving a finite-scale amplification theorem and linking various mathematical concepts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between mathematical interpolation theory and quantum physics through exact correspondence in the context of bosonic systems.

Method: A unitary Fischer--Bargmann realization is applied to relate degrees of homogeneous polynomials to particle numbers; vanishing orders correspond to dark states, leading to a theorem on no amplification of such states.

Result: The paper derives a finite-scale no-dark-state amplification theorem, confirms the relationship between algebraic constructs and quantum states, and concludes with an asymptotic result linking to Demailly's conjecture.

Conclusion: The individual components of the research are structured to allow independent reading, showing the interconnectedness of projective geometry, quantum mechanics, and number theory.

Abstract: We give an exact translation between fat-point interpolation in complex projective space and a dark-state problem for fixed-particle-number bosons. Under the unitary Fischer--Bargmann realization, homogeneous degree becomes particle number, the Veronese lift becomes a bosonic symmetric product state, and order-$m$ vanishing becomes orthogonality to all coherent jet excitations of orders below $m$. The initial degree of a symbolic power is therefore the first particle number admitting a dark state of a positive constraint Hamiltonian, and the Waldschmidt constant is the asymptotic particle cost per suppression order. After developing both sides of this dictionary from first principles, we prove a finite-scale no-dark-state amplification theorem. Frobenius amplifies the associated full-rank certificate in one good positive-characteristic fiber; a nonzero maximal minor returns it to characteristic zero. Its asymptotic consequence is Demailly's conjectured inequality. The paper is written so that the projective statement, the quantum reformulation, and the arithmetic part of the solution can be read independently and then identified exactly.

</details>


### [178] [Kinematic Control and Dephasing Dynamics of Quantum Resources in $e^{+}e^{-}\toτ^{+}τ^{-}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08555)
*Elhabib Jaloum,Omar Bachain,Mohamed Amazioug,Rachid Ahl Laamara*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper analyzes quantum resources in the spins of $τ^{+}τ^{-}$ pairs from $e^{+}e^{-}$ annihilation at Belle-II, revealing the importance of emission angle and environmental factors on quantum correlations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the quantum properties of spin states produced in particle collisions and their dependence on kinematic variables.

Method: Characterization of spin states as mixtures of separable and entangled components, and propagation through a correlated-dephasing channel to study the effects of environmental memory.

Result: Identified that spin correlations are maximized for transverse emission angles and environmental factors lead to collapses and revivals in quantum resources, differing from standard Markovian predictions.

Conclusion: The study distinctly separates the mechanisms creating spin correlations from the noise influences that affect their preservation.

Abstract: We characterize several quantum resources carried by the spins of the $τ^{+}τ^{-}$ pair produced in $e^{+}e^{-}$ annihilation. At the Belle-II energy, $\sqrt{s}=10.579\,\mathrm{GeV}$, Bell nonlocality, steerability, entanglement of formation, and coherence are governed by the production angle and are largest for transverse emission, $\vartheta=π/2$. Their common kinematic origin is exposed by expressing the spin state as a velocity and angle-dependent mixture of a separable contribution and a maximally entangled component. Within the physical production domain, this representation connects the weakly correlated threshold state at $\sqrt{s}=2m_τ$ to the Bell-state limit approached at ultrarelativistic energies. We then propagate the two-spin state through a phenomenological correlated-dephasing channel to determine how environmental memory and inter-channel classical correlations affect the available resources. Memory effects generate collapses and revivals that are absent from the monotonic Markovian evolution. The analysis therefore separates the kinematic mechanism that creates the spin correlations from the noise properties that control their subsequent survival.

</details>


### [179] [Boundary-Cancelled Score-Hamiltonian Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05904)
*Masayuki Ohzeki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper connects diffusion sampling with imaginary-time annealing via a Euclidean Schrödinger framework, revealing how score-learning errors relate to Hamiltonian perturbations and demonstrating improved sampling accuracy through specific boundary conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a deeper understanding of diffusion sampling and its relationship with Hamiltonian dynamics and score learning, aiming to improve sampling accuracy and reduce errors.

Method: Utilizing a forward/backward Euclidean Schrödinger framework, the study establishes a connection between score-learning errors and Hamiltonian perturbations, introducing a schedule principle based on terminal derivatives and using reverse-SDE benchmarks for validation.

Result: The analysis shows that fixing the noising drift enhances reverse score performance and can reduce residual Hellinger error from $T^{-2}$ to $T^{-2r-2}$, confirming the predicted improvements through benchmark tests.

Conclusion: The study effectively demonstrates that manipulating boundary conditions and understanding the interplay between score learning and Hamiltonians can significantly enhance sampling accuracy in diffusion processes.

Abstract: Diffusion sampling can be viewed as imaginary-time annealing of probability densities. From a forward/backward Euclidean Schrödinger pair, we show that fixing the noising drift forces the reverse score term, and that the same logarithmic force is the one-sided imaginary-time counterdiabatic connection of a supersymmetric Score Hamiltonian. The correspondence turns score-learning error into a Hamiltonian perturbation and yields a schedule principle: if the first $r$ terminal derivatives vanish, Morita--Nishimori boundary cancellation suppresses the residual Hellinger error from $T^{-2}$ to $T^{-2r-2}$ until score or sampling floors are reached. Reverse-SDE benchmarks, including two-dimensional learned-score tests, confirm the predicted improvement.

</details>


### [180] [Reducing quantum and classical resources for quantum-centric supercomputing workloads on near-term hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05957)
*Maxence Grandadam,Vladyslav Bohun,Dikshant Dulal,Maciej Koch-Janusz*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper evaluates Sample-based Krylov quantum diagonalization (SKQD) under depolarizing noise, highlighting the challenges posed by hardware noise on convergence, while presenting solutions through circuit compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation lies in optimizing quantum computing workflows, particularly SKQD, under practical noise constraints to ensure efficient quantum state sampling and convergence.

Method: The authors analyze the effect of depolarizing noise on SKQD, derive shot-count resource estimates, and test on a noisy quantum computer with the single-impurity Anderson model. They propose approximate compilation techniques for circuit compression.

Result: Experiments reveal that device noise disrupts expected convergence, but circuit compression techniques mitigate noise effects, allowing for improved energy convergence and reduced quantum resource requirements.

Conclusion: Compressed circuits are advantageous, enabling convergence recovery even with noise, thus making SKQD feasible for practical quantum computing applications.

Abstract: Sample-based Krylov quantum diagonalization (SKQD) is a paradigmatic example of a quantum-centric supercomputing workflow that combines the convergence structure of Krylov quantum diagonalization with classical sampling-based post-processing. It provides convergence guarantees assuming that the important computational-basis configurations can be sampled from a set of Krylov states with sufficient probability. We analyze this assumption under depolarizing noise, deriving shot-count resource estimates that expose an exponential depth penalty, and perform experiments on current noisy hardware with the one-dimensional single-impurity Anderson model on a 20-site (40-qubit) instance. Device noise breaks the practical convergence predicted by the noiseless SKQD analysis, but approximate compilation techniques can compress the Krylov time-evolution circuits before execution. The compressed circuits accumulate less hardware noise, recover the expected energy convergence, and reduce both the quantum shot budget and the classical subspace dimension, remaining beneficial even when classical configuration recovery is applied.

</details>


### [181] [Data-driven structural diagnostics and autonomous alignment of complex optical systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05984)
*Peng Zhu,Chen Chen,Shuchang Ma,Dezhou Deng,J. F. Chen,Peng Chen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a data-driven framework for the quick autonomous alignment and maintenance of complex optical systems using a high-finesse optical cavity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is an urgent need for efficient alignment and maintenance methods in free-space optical systems for large-scale quantum processors.

Method: A high-finesse optical cavity is used as a sensitive probe within a data-driven framework that enables rapid response and control.

Result: Achieves cold-start convergence in alignment and maintenance within seconds, indicating effectiveness in managing complex optical architectures.

Conclusion: This methodology provides a scalable solution for autonomous operation in free-space optical systems.

Abstract: The quest for autonomous alignment and maintenance of complex free-space optical systems is increasingly urgent for large-scale neutral-atom quantum processors. Leveraging a high-finesse optical cavity as a sensitive probe, we introduce a data-driven framework for structural diagnostics and closed-loop control that achieves cold-start convergence within tens of seconds. These results establish a rapid, scalable diagnostic-control methodology for autonomous alignment and continuous maintenance of complex free-space optical architectures.

</details>


### [182] [The $I_{3322}$ Bell inequality requires infinite dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06038)
*Andrea Coladangelo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves the conjecture that the $I_{3322}$ Bell inequality can be maximally violated using an infinite-dimensional quantum strategy, while finite-dimensional strategies cannot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the conjecture regarding the unique properties of the $I_{3322}$ Bell inequality and its relation to infinite versus finite-dimensional quantum strategies.

Method: The authors provide a formal proof that demonstrates the conjectured property of the $I_{3322}$ inequality, building upon an approximate proof generated through interactions with AI.

Result: The proof verifies that no finite-dimensional quantum strategy can achieve the maximal violation of the $I_{3322}$ inequality, confirming the existing conjecture.

Conclusion: The findings establish the validity of the conjecture and clarify the distinction between finite and infinite-dimensional quantum strategies in relation to the $I_{3322}$ inequality.

Abstract: The $I_{3322}$ inequality is one of the simplest bipartite Bell inequalities, with three possible questions and two possible answers per party. Yet, despite its simplicity, it has long been conjectured by Pál and Vértesi (Physical Review A, 2010) to possess the following quirk: no finite-dimensional quantum strategy can attain its maximal violation, but an infinite-dimensional strategy can. A Bell correlation, with five questions and three answers per party, that provably possesses the same property has since been discovered by Coladangelo and Stark (Nature Communications, 2020). However, proving that the same property holds for $I_{3322}$, which lives in the simplest Bell scenario in which such a phenomenon could occur, has remained elusive. Here, we provide a proof of this conjecture.
  An approximate proof, containing all the main ideas, was produced by GPT 5.5 Pro after various rounds of interaction. The presentation of this proof was revised substantially by the author for completeness, correctness, and clarity.

</details>


### [183] [Propagating sparsely supported states with time-dependent neural quantum states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06054)
*Lexin Ding,Markus Reiher*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces interpolation sampling to improve propagation of quantum states in time-dependent neural quantum states (NQSs), allowing for accurate representation of peaked states that traditional methods struggle with. It also enhances sampling efficiency through joint sampling and hybrid strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing time-dependent NQS methods struggle with sparsely supported initial states due to a mismatch with their time derivatives.

Method: The paper introduces interpolation sampling, combining wave function and its time derivative, along with a global-in-time variational principle and improved sampling techniques.

Result: Demonstrates accurate propagation of extremely peaked states in the two-dimensional transverse-field Ising model, overcoming limitations of standard sampling methods.

Conclusion: The method broadens the applicability of time-dependent NQS techniques and questions the reliance on traditional Born distribution for sampling.

Abstract: Neural quantum states (NQSs) have emerged as a powerful ansatz for quantum dynamics. Their high entanglement capacity promises to overcome the entanglement barrier. However, existing time-dependent NQS methods are ill-equipped to propagate sparsely supported initial states due to the inherent mismatch between the supports of such states and their time derivatives. To address this issue, we introduce the interpolation sampling method as a novel form of importance sampling, where samples are drawn from a distribution that interpolates between the wave function and its time derivative. Combined with a global-in-time variational principle, we demonstrate with the example of the two-dimensional transverse-field Ising model that interpolation sampling allows for accurate propagation of extremely peaked states that standard wave function sampling could not tackle. We further improve sampling efficiency by introducing (i) a configuration-time joint sampling scheme where spin configurations and time are both treated as random variables, and (ii) a hybrid strategy for sample proposal that incorporates the knowledge of a Krylov subspace. Our work extends the scope of time-dependent NQS methods to a wider range of physically relevant scenarios, while challenging the prevailing reliance on the Born distribution and its close variants as the default basis for sampling.

</details>


### [184] [Unraveling-Dependent Metastability in Monitored Quantum Systems and Associative Memories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06056)
*Manali Malakar,Roberta Zambrini,Gian Luca Giorgi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper reveals that metastability in open quantum systems is not solely determined by ensemble-averaged dynamics; individual trajectories can exhibit different metastable behaviors based on monitoring conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limitations of using spectral separation in Liouvillian dynamics for diagnosing metastability in open quantum systems, particularly in individual quantum trajectories.

Method: The authors analyze a driven-dissipative nonlinear oscillator functioning as a quantum associative memory, comparing spectra, target fidelities, and phase-space distributions under various monitoring conditions.

Result: The study shows that the behavior of metastability can differ significantly at the trajectory level, revealing distinct dynamics influenced by monitoring channels and measurement records.

Conclusion: Trajectory-level metastability requires a compatibility between the monitoring channel, observed records, and the metastable structure, indicating that averaged dynamics alone do not capture the full picture.

Abstract: Metastability in open quantum systems is usually inferred from spectral separation in the Liouvillian, which governs unconditional, ensemble-averaged dynamics. We show that this diagnosis is incomplete at the level of individual quantum trajectories: conditioned realizations of the same unconditional dynamics can bypass, transiently access, or operationally preserve a metastable memory, depending on the monitored channel and the observed record. We demonstrate these mechanisms in a driven-dissipative nonlinear oscillator realizing a quantum associative memory, by comparing spectra, target fidelities, and phase-space distributions. The non-Hermitian dynamics obtained by post-selecting on the absence of detected events supports metastable retrieval, but with a distinct long-time fate: normalization selects the least-decaying mode of the non-Hermitian spectrum rather than the addressed memory branch. In contrast, stochastic jump trajectories can preserve retrieval over extended times when the post-measurement update remains compatible with the coherent memory structure. Thus trajectory-level metastability is not determined by the averaged generator alone; it requires compatibility between the monitored channel, the measurement record, and the metastable manifold.

</details>


### [185] [Battery Locality Is Necessary in Noncommuting Quantum Charging Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06104)
*Sheryl Mathew*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that battery locality is essential for quantum batteries, particularly for spin batteries with noncommuting interactions, which can exceed typical charging bounds due to their unique dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve whether battery locality is operationally necessary in quantum batteries, especially given constraints in charging power due to locality of the charging Hamiltonian.

Method: Constructed both commuting and noncommuting high-locality spin batteries with identical interaction supports and local energy scales, utilizing the same charging protocol.

Result: The commuting battery hit the locality-independent bound while the noncommuting battery exceeded it parametrically, showcasing that the locality constraint does not universally apply.

Conclusion: Battery locality, facilitated by noncommuting interactions, is established as an important operational resource in quantum charging protocols.

Abstract: Bounds on the charging power of quantum batteries in direct charging protocols are often interpreted as charging-side constraints. Although the locality of the charging Hamiltonian is known to restrict attainable power in certain protocols, whether battery locality is itself operationally necessary has remained unresolved. Here we show that it is necessary for spin batteries whose interactions do not pairwise commute. We construct commuting and noncommuting high-locality batteries with identical interaction supports and local energy scale, driven by the same charging. The commuting battery saturates the battery-locality-independent bound, whereas the noncommuting battery exceeds it by a parametrically growing factor, demonstrating that this bound cannot apply generally. The enhanced norm of the commutator is dynamically attained from a suitable joint state supported entirely in the battery ground-energy sector, although this state may be correlated with the auxiliary fermionic degrees of freedom. Our results establish battery locality as an operational resource enabled by noncommuting battery interactions.

</details>


### [186] [Measurement-Based Feedback of Open Quantum Systems: A Control-Theoretic Review and Tutorial](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06111)
*Weichao Liang,Jing Zhang,Gaoyue Guo,Daoyi Dong*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This review addresses measurement-based feedback in continuously monitored open quantum systems, focusing on finite-dimensional systems governed by stochastic master equations, while discussing control-theoretic frameworks and implementation challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To connect measurement-based quantum feedback with control theory, exploring how measurements influence system dynamics and optimal control strategies.

Method: The review employs a control-theoretic framework, analyzing state-space structures, quantum filtering as observer dynamics, and providing a recurrence-contraction framework for Hamiltonian feedback.

Result: The paper elucidates critical concepts in open-loop asymptotics, filter stability, state-feedback stabilization, and highlights how these concepts apply to measurement-induced dynamics in quantum systems.

Conclusion: By framing measurement-based quantum control within established control theory concepts, the review enhances understanding and paves the way for addressing practical challenges in quantum system implementations.

Abstract: This review develops a control-theoretic perspective on measurement-based feedback for continuously monitored open quantum systems, with the main analysis focused on finite-dimensional systems governed by diffusive stochastic master equations. We introduce the relevant state-space, invariant-subspace, and quantum non-demolition structures, interpret quantum filtering as nonlinear observer dynamics, and review open-loop asymptotics, filter stability, state-feedback stabilization, robustness, and reduced-order observer-based control. Particular emphasis is placed on a recurrence--contraction framework, in which Hamiltonian feedback removes non-target invariant obstructions while measurement-induced dynamics provide local exponential contraction. Although the detailed analysis is developed for finite-dimensional diffusive models, the underlying measurement--estimation--feedback architecture is relevant across a broad range of quantum platforms. We further discuss implementation challenges and open problems involving scalable estimation, sampling and delay, adaptation, hybrid and non-Markovian dynamics, practical stability, optimal control, and learning-based design. By organizing these developments around invariance, estimation, recurrence, and contraction, the review provides a tutorial bridge between measurement-based quantum feedback and nonlinear stochastic control.

</details>


### [187] [The Future-Record Model of Everettian Probability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06130)
*Jonathan Baxter*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper extends a previously proposed model of Everettian probability to include all decoherent states, not just those with observer continuations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore a more comprehensive definition of objective Everettian probability by considering a broader set of physical records.

Method: The model is expanded from earlier work to encompass all decoherent record states using observer-indexed and exhaustive physical-record models.

Result: The extended model allows for a clearer understanding of decoherent physical records, broadening the scope of Everettian probability.

Conclusion: The observer-indexed model serves as a subset of the more inclusive exhaustive physical-record model, enhancing theoretical frameworks in quantum probability.

Abstract: I recently showed how objective Everettian probability can be defined over the records borne by an observer's later continuations, without unique actualization or additional premeasurement subjects \citep{Baxter2026}. Here I extend that model to all decoherent physical record states, including records that support no such continuation. The observer-indexed model is then a restriction of an exhaustive physical-record model.

</details>


### [188] [Quantifying Nonstabilizerness of Quantum Codes by Removing the Inert Background](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06177)
*Yuan Liu,Ke-Mi Xu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper provides a new method to quantify nonstabilizerness in quantum codes by focusing on the inherent structure of Clifford transformations, leading to simplified formulas and bounds for various quantum code families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding nonstabilizerness is crucial for advancements in quantum computation and quantifying its presence in quantum codes has been a challenging problem.

Method: The authors analyze the Clifford sector's contribution to nonstabilizerness and derive an evaluable fourth moment after removing this sector, simplifying the calculation for various code families.

Result: They obtain closed-form expressions and bounds for the nonstabilizerness of specific code families, significantly reducing computational complexity from exponential to polynomial times.

Conclusion: The results enable the quantification of nonstabilizerness across a wide range of quantum codes and identify the most 'magical' codes, making further research into quantum computing more tractable.

Abstract: Nonstabilizerness (magic) is the quantum resource that, together with stabilizer operations, makes universal quantum computation possible. It appears in fault-tolerant codes and topological order. However, quantifying nonstabilizerness is difficult. The standard measure sums over exponentially many Pauli operators, and for quantum codes no quantitative theory has been available. We resolve this by a structural observation: the Clifford sector carries no magic and can be removed by a Clifford transformation, leaving an order-three object whose magic is an exactly evaluable fourth moment. Removing this inert background yields closed forms for several code families: a formula for all Dicke states, reducing a 100-qubit case from $4^{100}$ terms to a short binomial sum; a bound for cubic-phase codes (twisted quantum doubles and non-Abelian topological order), saturated only by the $D_4$ code; and a cyclic/zero criterion for group multiplication states. Together these results reduce the computation of nonstabilizerness for a broad class of codes to finite classical counting problems, and identify the maximally magical codes among them.

</details>


### [189] [Quantum lattice Boltzmann method via density-matrix encoding for fluid simulation with wall boundary conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06179)
*Hao Su,Boyuan Wang,Yue Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The proposed quantum lattice Boltzmann method (QLBM) efficiently addresses complex boundary conditions in fluid simulations through enhanced algorithms for wall boundaries and velocity conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenges of incorporating realistic solid wall boundaries in quantum fluid simulations using QLBM.

Method: A QLBM algorithm is introduced that uses density-matrix encoding and incorporates a half-way bounce-back scheme for boundary conditions, alongside controlled SWAP operations for inlet/outlet conditions.

Result: The algorithm is validated through accurate simulations of various two-dimensional fluid flows, demonstrating its effectiveness in handling complex geometries.

Conclusion: The proposed QLBM provides a robust solution for implementing realistic boundary conditions in quantum fluid simulations, showcasing accuracy and extensibility.

Abstract: The quantum lattice Boltzmann method (QLBM) holds promise for efficient fluid simulations, yet the treatment of realistic boundary conditions, particularly solid wall boundary with arbitrary geometry, remains a critical open challenge. We propose a QLBM algorithm that incorporates wall boundaries as well as inlet/outlet velocity conditions. Building upon the existing QLBM with ensemble transformations, we adopt a density-matrix encoding that offers greater flexibility in circuit design and enables the implementation of non-unitary operations through Kraus operators. For boundary enforcement, we design a quantum implementation of the half-way bounce-back scheme and introduce a component exchange step prior to the streaming step, which naturally enforces the no-slip condition without modifying the transport operation. Inlet and outlet conditions are imposed via controlled SWAP operations combined with ancilla registers prepared in the prescribed velocity states.
